Cultural Criticism
An increasing number of methods in one way or another hinge on
the assumption that both texts and interpretations reflect their cultures
and are often grouped under the heading of cultural studies. Texts and
their interpreters are at the same time both products and producers of
culture in terms of a mix of cultural systems such as art, economics,
race, gender, sexual orientation, and politics. In other words, since texts,
interpretations, and interpreters are cultural phenomena, the writing and
interpreting of texts will never be independent of cultural forces and
systems within which human communities operate. According to cul-
tural critics, therefore, methods that claim objectivity by bracketing out
or simply ignoring these cultural systems are faulty from the beginning
because they fail to make room for the fundamental fact that all human
activity and understanding take place under the pressure of culture.
Since so many methods have developed out of the focus on the role
that culture plays in the writing and interpretive process, only a few rep-
resentative methods are presented below . The resources listed contain
a more exhaustive treatment of these methods that focus on the world
304 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS

--- Page 326 ---
in front of the text as it relates to the influence of culture on the enter -
prise of interpretation.
Adam, A. K. M., ed. Handbook of Postmodern Biblical Interpretation. St.
Louis: Chalice, 2000.
Hayes, John, ed.The Dictionary of Biblical Interpretation.Nashville: Abing-
don, 1999.
T a t e ,W .R a n d o l p h .Interpreting the Bible: A Handbook of Terms and Methods .
Peabody, Mass.: Hendrickson, 2006.
In these texts the reader will find essays on Postcolonial feminist
criticism, gender criticism, race criticism, mujerista criticism, Asian/
Asian American criticism, queer theory, gay/lesbian criticism, materi-
alist criticism, and socio-economic criticism, to name a few .
Deconstruction
Hermeneutics is, for him [Derrida], the process whereby we encounter
the ambiguity in texts and in all cultural products and find in the very am-
biguity new possibilities for thought and action.
20
Jacques Derrida, the French philosopher most associated with de-
construction, consciously rejects any reference to his work as a system.
Moreover, in order to militate against the tendency of scholars to de-
fine his work as a system, Derrida has adopted a strategy of employing
terms in such an ambiguous and inconsistent manner that his critical
thought is not easily classified by terminology. In order to speak at least
coherently about deconstruction, however, I concentrate on select terms
(used by Derrida and other deconstruction critics) that will assist in
characterizing deconstruction.
Almost central to Derrida’s thought on meaning is his concept of
“writing” (écriture). “Writing” for Derrida is not the inscription of thought,
but the fundamental human activity which precedes such inscription.
In most Western hermeneutical thinking, individuals are able to write
a text because they have previously “read the world.” Or put another
way, the world of reality that we variously encode within our texts al-
ready exists as a metaphysical reality. Derrida rejects this assumption
by claiming that the world we are capable of knowing comes into
being through the act of “writing.” There is no world waiting to be
Methods that Focus on the World in Front of the Text 305
20 Walhout, “Texts and Actions,” 35.

--- Page 327 ---
read. Language is not rooted in a perceivable reality that precedes it; on
the contrary, language itself sets the parameters of thought and, there-
fore, our world. If this is the case, we cannot be aware of the “presence”
of that which is beyond our thoughts, but only of its “absence.”
21
In Derrida’s estimation, since all human activity takes place in
time, such activity is historically bound. Now this does not mean that
human activity is solely historically or culturally determined. Human
activity also determines and perpetually reshapes and redefines cul -
ture. Culture does not exist prior to individual acts of “writing.” Prior
assumptions about reality may exist, but these are themselves products
of cultural “writings.” This is the reason Derrida can assert that “writ-
ing” precedes speech. It is simply impossible to know something which
exists prior to what is known.
Two other terms which help to explain the concept of “writing”
are trace and mark. Each and every human action leaves a mark on cul-
ture, and each and every mark gives witness to its originating human
action, an action which itself escapes complete specification. There-
fore, writing’s mark leaves a trace of the indeterminate origin of the
writing.
If every human action has both a culturally specific mark and a
trace of the moment of origination, then every human action has not
only a determinate meaning, but also an indeterminate meaning. More
simply put, if every human action is shaped by culture, while at the
same time shaping the culture, the culture is constantly and infinitely
undergoing modification and change while in a reciprocal fashion in-
fluencing the actions that change it. This complex of mutual influence
gives rise to both continuity and discontinuity between the action and
the culture. Therefore, interpretations of human actions (including lit-
erary ones) must focus on both the way in which an action is shaped by
culture (i.e., the action’s continuity with the culture) and the way in
which an action is discontinuous with the culture.
This brings us to another of Derrida’s focal terms—différance. This
word is a deliberate misspelling of the word différence, combining two
other words—differing and deferring. Using Saussure’s definition of sign,
Derrida says that a text derives meaning in itsdifferencesfrom other texts.
But every text also evidences the trace of an indeterminate origination.
Since this indeterminate quality creates change within the culture, mean-
ing is alwaysdeferred. Interpretation itself becomes an activity laden with
ambiguity. Interpretation is a human action with a determinate and in-
306 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS
21 Ibid., 36.

--- Page 328 ---
determinate meaning. Every hermeneut approaches a text within a cul-
ture, both influenced by and exerting influence upon the culture. Since
this is an infinite process of shaping and reshaping, interpretation is
an endless, ever-changing process. Interpretation is an infinite process
of creation. All meaning is bound by cultural context, but that context
becomes boundless.
Deconstruction critics assume that language, rather than being trans-
parent, is inherently ambiguous, metaphorical, and rhetorical. Conse-
quently, a text as a linguistic object is not a unified and coherent whole,
but radically rhetorical and metaphorical. Actually, deconstruction is
more a way of analyzing interpretive methods and philosophical sys -
tems than a method of interpretation itself. It is a backward analysis of
the historical development of a tradition so as to arrive at its hidden
presuppositions and bases. Derrida is interested in deconstructing West-
ern philosophy and its metaphysical foundations.
If deconstruction critics are correct in their claims that all language
is inherently metaphorical and that there exists no metaphysical reality
outside our “writing,” then all hermeneutics which seek a grounding
either in linguistic objectivity or in a world of reality beyond language,
of which language is simply the linguistic representation, are like the
proverbial house built upon the sand. But do we really have a choice?
Can we communicate (i.e., think and speak) in any other way except
through language? Can we interpret outside the confines of some sys-
tem? The answer to these questions must be negative. Nevertheless,
while we may interpret within some system, we should take the re-
minders of the deconstruction critics in a constructive sense. At best,
interpretive systems rest on theoretical bases. We should be willing to
subject our methodological bases to scrutiny, for if the base is faulty, so
are our subsequent interpretations.

New Historicism
New historicism developed in the United States in the 1980s. It
was most succinctly defined by Stephen Greenblatt in 1982 22 and has
spread to several fields of study, including biblical studies. Catherine
Gallagher suggests that both adherents and opponents of the theory
would probably agree that new historicism views literary and nonliter-
ary texts as “constituents of historical discourses that are both inside
and outside of texts and that its practitioners generally posit no fixed
308 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS
22 Stephen J. Greenblatt, Renaissance Self-Fashioning: From More to Shake-
speare (Chicago: University of Chicago Press), 1980.

--- Page 330 ---
hierarchy of cause and effect as they trace the connections among
texts, discourses, power, and the constitution of subjectivity.”23
Historical criticisms that have recently rejected the ahistorical criti-
cal approaches that developed with New Criticism have been heavily
influenced by Marxist criticism, deconstruction, and postmodern criti-
cism. These historical approaches distinguish between history (the past)
and historiography (the writing about the past) and reject the older
historicists’ claim that they can objectively reconstruct the past by read-
ing its artifacts. While new historicism cannot be classified as a single,
unified critical approach (some critics refer to their work as sociological
poetics, others as cultural studies, still others as cultural criticism), the
critics who practice it do share some common concerns, including the
subjectivity of history, the historian’s and the critic’s ideologically tainted
situation in writing and reading, the relationship between historiogra -
phy and fiction, the relationship between discourse and ideology, and
the textuality of the past.
First, according to new historicists, all history is subjective. Those
who are dominant in culture tend to write it, suppressing the minority
voices and leaving a biased record of the events. Consequently, new
historicism presupposes the “historicity” of texts and the “textuality
of history.” By the former is meant that all texts are both written and
read within historically and ideologically determined contexts. The lat-
ter claim means that the only access that readers or historians have to
the past is through texts, which are themselves subject to the pressure
of ideological context. Consequently, there is no direct access to the
past, only that one mediated by texts.
Second, the new historicists see a direct connection between a text
and its socio-historical context. A text of any kind is a product of so -
cial causes as well as a cause of social effects. Histories are not objec -
tive reconstructions of the past but are textual constructs of critics and
historians within specific historical and literary institutions. As such,
literary texts do not transcend their socio-historical contexts to tap into
some transcendent realm of mimetic truth and knowledge. These critics
are keenly aware that we cannot examine the past except through the
lenses of the present. Neither historians nor literary critics occupy some
objective, transcendent position from which to examine the past. They
cannot find any natural, non-historical sources for their foundational
Methods that Focus on the World in Front of the Text 309
23 Catharine Gallagher, “Marxism and New Historicism,” in New His-
toricism and Cultural Materialism (ed. Ryan Kiernan; London: Arnold, 1996),
45.

--- Page 331 ---
assumptions. They can discover no natural givens, only their own his-
torically constructed ideas that have no essence, only a history. Histori-
ans, literary artists, critics, and readers are all implicated in the act of
writing and/or reading. Since texts are written and interpreted within
specific historical, sociopolitical, and literary institutions, the histories
and interpretations of texts are as much constructs by historically con-
structed subjects as are the past objects to be examined. This dilemma
is complicated by the fact that since the original contexts of past events
and texts cannot be fully reconstructed, historians and critics must con-
struct interpretive contexts.
Third, new historicists tend to blur the line between historiography
and fiction, viewing the former as the narrative arrangement of past
events. Since historians must supply the contexts for most past events
and objects as well as include select events while excluding others, an
account of the past is always a misrepresentation. A text’s context is it-
self a construct of the historian or critic. In other words, the historian/
critic constructs a context and then assumes that it is a representation of
the circumstances of the text’s composition. From this perspective, then,
the contextual communities (e.g., Matthean community, Essene com-
munity, Johannine community, or Priestly community) which scholars
employ to say something meaningful about biblical texts are fictional
creations of the scholars themselves. Historians, critics, and readers of
all sorts fail to make a distinction between the actual past and their
representations of the past. In other words, there is a fundamental
misconception that their constructions are reconstructions. The new
historicists, recognizing this basic epistemological blind spot, argue that
the literary products of historians and scholars are just as fictive as
texts that profess to be such.
The fourth concern of new historicism is the relationship between
discourse and ideology. Historical and critical discourses are soundly
rooted in ideologies. Rather than being objective, reflecting the way
things were, accounts of the past reflect a particular present ideological
perspective. In other words, they are attempts to legitimate the present
or at least some aspect of it. A discourse is what an institution defines
as truth, and this definition is an agreed-upon set of operational prac -
tices that themselves are beyond question or scrutiny and circumscribe
the knowledge presented by the historian or critic. Since historians and
critics are located within ideologies (socioeconomic, political, theologi-
cal), historical and critical analyses of the past or of texts are not value-
free. Such an assumption militates against the possibility of objective
historical narratives and introduces into the practice of historiography
310 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS

--- Page 332 ---
and criticism the issue of ethical practice. Both historical and literary
texts display and enforce existing rules of conduct, etiquette, and laws.
Literary texts and their interpreters do not transcend the historical mo-
ment but are inevitably part of a complex ideological grounding. What
past historians and critics have assumed to be universals (e.g., gender,
race, and sexuality) are in reality social and historical constructs. In
other words, both historical and literary texts are implicated in ideo -
logical agendas. It is clear, then, that new historicism is deconstructive
in that it has been quite effective in challenging foundationalist and
essentialist claims, deconstructing ideologies, and demonstrating that
almost everything that humans consider to be universal, natural, or
timeless is actually historically contingent and socially constructed.
Historical criticism has been a part of the study of biblical texts for
years. Two aspects that unify new historicists in biblical interpretation
are a rejection of traditional approaches and “a fundamental question-
ing of the kind of historiography represented both by the writings in
the Bible and by those contemporary biblical historians whose books
may be regarded as the continuation of the biblical narratives by other
means.”
24 New historicists believe that a new approach is needed that
focuses on the subjectivity of history and on the marginalized voices
that have been left out by the utopian religious viewpoints of the bibli-
cal writers. For instance, historicists studying the Pauline Letters have
constructed ideas of what the communities were like that received them
(Corinthians, Ephesians, etc.). New historicists claim that it is impos-
sible to know what those cultures were like because they can only be ac-
cessed through secondhand historicity and textuality . They also claim
that the accounts of ancient Israel’s history have silenced other cul -
tures which may have actually been more dominant, such as Palestine.
Ultimately, many new historicists want repressed texts that are discov-
ered (e.g., Nag Hamaddi) to be included in the canon, a goal that
would be seriously opposed by many Christians.

Postcolonial Criticism & Liberation Theology
As the literary child of postcolonialism, postcolonial criticism fo-
cuses on the relationship between literature, literary canon, and im-
perialism. It emphasizes diversity as each critic brings his or her own
emphasis to the discourses that center on several basic assumptions
before branching out into the rich multiplicity inherent in this theory.
A basic assumption of postcolonial critics is that the dominating cul -
ture wrote the literature and decided what literature was canonized.
This literature, even the very language that was used, affected society
by propagating the differentiation and subsequent oppression of the
colonized.
25
This basic assumption dictates to postcolonialists their main tasks,
the first of which Alfred J. López calls “a reckoning with the colonial
past.”
26 This task involves the critique of the Western assumptions of
what literature has been canonized as well as the reinterpretation of
312 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS
25 Donald E. Hall, Literary and Cultural Theory: From Basic Principles to
Advanced Applications (Boston: Houghton Mifflin, 2001), 268–69.
26 Alfred J. López, Posts and Pasts: A Theory of Postcolonialism (Albany:
State University of New York Press, 2001), 7.

--- Page 334 ---
the texts with an awareness of their imperialist assumptions. The sec -
ond task consists of a call for due attention to be given to the literature
of marginalized people. And the third task is to discuss postcolonial
diasporas, pan-nationalism, and nationalism.
27 This task includes dis-
courses on the production of culture and the complexity of identity—
the inherent hybridity of both the colonist and the subjugated people.
Postcolonialists employ many theoretical approaches, most often psycho-
analysis and deconstruction, and are commonly influenced by Marxist
and feminist criticism.
Edward Said, one of the founders of postcolonialist thought, is
concerned with the duty of the critic in the face of the Eurocentrism
and cultural elitism of literary scholarship. He argues that the task of
the critic is “oppositional criticism” against domination, for the goal of
“noncoercive knowledge in the interests of human freedom.”
28 Said’s
most significant contribution to the field is his book Orientalism,i n
which he examines Western images of the Orient—both in the aca-
demic disciplines, such as anthropology, and in popular representation
in movies, literature, etc.
29 Said states that these representations of the
oriental “other” are projections of the West, having little to do with
the actual nature of the people represented. These constructions foster
biases and justify Western domination of third-world cultures.
30
This theme of imperialist representations of other cultures is com-
mon among postcolonial theorists. Gerald Vizenor directs similar at-
tention to the portrayal of Native Americans in the academic world and
popular culture. This portrayal “proved” the Native Americans’ natural
inferiority and helped to justify the ideology of “manifest destiny”—
an ideology of spreading over the continent and subjugating the Native
Americans.
31
A residual phenomenon arising in the wake of colonialism, cul -
tural imperialism encompasses the effects of colonization in language,

In her controversial essay “Can the Subaltern Speak,” Gayarti Spi-
vak levels criticism at postcolonialist efforts at emancipating cultures.
Spivak claims that postcolonialists can easily be condescending to the
people to whom they are claiming to give a voice. They do this in the
very notion that third-world cultures must lead the fight against cultural
imperialism, assigning them value only as far as they follow Western
scripts.
33 Theorists can also do this by romanticizing the oppressed,
essentializing them, and replicating the colonists they criticize.
Spivak’s pragmatic application of her theories to real-life political
(in her case, feminist) goals is characteristic of many postcolonial theo-
rists. It seems to be common among these critics to see art as propa-
gating social values. They often go beyond recommending academic
measures (such as Achebe’s call for writing from the perspective of the
oppressed and Ngugi’s call for the abolition of the English depart-
ment) to advocate practical political steps. Homi Bhabha asserts that
theory should be accompanied by political activism, not separated from
it,
34 and Edward Said relates his criticism to real-world politics and
is strategically involved with the Palestinian situation. 35 Literary and
social commentary is often accompanied by social action in the field
of Postcolonialism.
Postcolonial criticism has deep significance in how we view litera-
ture and society, and its value has been extended to other disciplines,
including the field of biblical studies. Interpretive theory in the field of
biblical studies has traditionally been dominated by Western interpre -

biblical studies. There are many examples of these readings from people
outside the center, such as Native American and African-American
readings, as well as a host of readings from people groups all over the
world (e.g., the contemporary Jewish reading of Exodus).

the Israelites’ exodus from Egypt. Usually the marginalized or oppressed

ironically be related to the ancient Israelites in this marginalized read-
ing. This has profound ramifications on the theology regarding Israel
and is an example of the rich possibilities of decentralizing our biblical
interpretations.

Reception Theory
Hans Robert Jauss, the principle architect of reception theory, claims
that in order to get a new reading of a text, the reader must step outside
his or her own historical praxis and view the work without any precon-
ceived notions.
42 However, Jauss believes such a step is virtually impos-
sible because a reader comes to the text with a certain horizon of
expectation. The traditional focus on historical objectivism must be re-
placed by an approach to literature that prioritizes issues of reception
rather than the impact on the reader.
For Jauss, the author, work, and reader form a fundamental tri -
angle in the literary process, and current criticism gives import to the
reader and the manner in which the reader is influenced by history. For
Jauss, the reader (the public) is more than “a chain of mere reactions”;
Methods that Focus on the World in Front of the Text 317
42 Hans Robert Jauss, The Aesthetic Experience and Literary Hermeneutics
(trans. Michael Shaw; vol. 3 of Theory and History of Literature ; Minneapolis:
University of Minnesota Press, 1982).

--- Page 339 ---
the reader is a formative part of history, and the literary work has no
historical life without the active participation of its audiences. 43 The
reader judges the aesthetic value of the work by comparing it to previ-
ously read texts. The latter historical implication lies in the observation
that the understandings of the first readers will be inscribed in the re -
ceptions of future generations.
According to Jauss, history is mere opinion. For example, if his -
tory (or, better, historiography) is objective, all of the gospels should be
exactly the same. The fact that the gospels are not the same but rather
have irreconcilable differences does not mean that one or more of the
texts is wrong. Jauss views history as a perspective of a certain group
or individual. Consequently, history is in no way objective but is jaded
by the perspective of the writer.
Jauss offers seven theses in his description of the aesthetics of re -
ception.
44 The first deals with the problem of objectivity in reading a
literary work: As a reader engages a text, preconceived ideas (originat-
ing from a number of sources—past text, status, economics, etc.)
45 are
present in either the reader’s conscience or subconscience. In other
words, certain receptions of past reading experiences are triggered with
each new reading, and these past experiences in turn influence any lit-
erary work a public reads. A literary work does not exist objectively by
itself, offering to every reader in every period the same view . A work is
more like an orchestration that strikes each reader differently. The his-
torical contexts of both work and reader create a horizon of expecta-
tion, a horizon which the reader believes will happen based upon his or
her historical background. Consequently, the possibility of compre -
hending and representing a literary work in its unique historicity is de-
pendent upon the objectification of the horizon of expectation.
Jauss’s second thesis concerns the manner in which one’s horizon
of expectation is continually evolving. What a reader expects a text to
do will change as the reader reads other texts and accumulates more
life experience. This change also applies to the rereading of a text.
When certain understandings are established in the first reading, a sub-
sequent reading allows for new questions to be raised and old lingering
questions to be answered. For example, a person studying the Gospel

ory,” in Critical Theory since 1965 (ed. Hazard Adams and Leroy Searle; Talla-
hassee: University of Florida Press, 1986), 164.
44 Ibid., 165.
45 Ibid., 166.

of Mark with little critical reading background may sense an uneasi -
ness with the manner in which the author portrays the disciples. If this
same reader then reads a book or books that point out the literary role
that the imperceptiveness of the disciples plays, not only will old ques-
tions be answered, but new ones will be generated, only to be answered
upon further critical reading.
The third thesis argues that since readers and the conditions within
which readers engage a text change, the text will have different effects
at any historical moment. A work that is intended for a specific society
at a specific time will not mean the same thing to that society twenty
years later. Jauss calls this phenomenon the horizontal change. But the
opposite can also happen. An author may write a work not intended for
a particular audience but have a significant influence on a later society .
For example, numerous texts in the Hebrew Bible had a formative im-
pact upon the writers of the New Testament.
The fourth thesis concerns the reconstruction of the horizon of ex-
pectation and the production of a literary work. Reconstructing the
horizon of expectation of the author and contemporary audience en-
ables the contemporary reader to discover how he or she should view
and understand the work.
46 It points up the hermeneutic difference be-
tween the past and the current understanding of the text. Jauss ar-
gues that a reader who is not part of the author’s original audience
must learn what the horizon of expectation might have been for the
original audience by considering the reading and writing style of the pe-
riod and the social, environmental, geographical, religious, and politi-
cal contexts.
The fifth thesis points out the evolution of the horizon of expecta -
tion of the text. After a reader’s theories and expectations have been
upset, many questions may possibly arise that the text has not answered.
As a reader engages other texts, however, these original questions may
be answered and new meaning found in the first text. Thus, a text can be
read hundreds of times with meaning always evolving.
In his sixth thesis, Jauss argues that not only does a text change over
time for a particular reader; it also takes on new meaning with each gen-
eration. One generation passes down its understanding of the text, with
the next generation beginning where the preceding one ended. Conse-
quently, a text’s meaning is always evolving diachronically across time.
In his seventh thesis, Jauss further develops his idea that social influ-
ences modify interpretations. Every social influence (from government
Methods that Focus on the World in Front of the Text 319
46 Ibid., 171.

--- Page 341 ---
programs to job pressures) has an impact on interpretation. While gen-
eral assumptions may be made dependent upon the major factors in a
reader’s life, the plethora of influencing factors precludes any predict -
ability of the way a reader will interpret a text at any given moment.
It is obvious that Jauss based his theory on the triangle of interpre-
tation, the points of which represent the three major constituents of
interpretation—author, text, and reader. The author has his or her own
interpretation of the text, the text holds the possibility of any number
of interpretations, and the audience may create further interpretations.
For Jauss, since each point of the triangle plays a significant part in the
interpretive process, no point should be neglected or prioritized in any
act of interpretation.

Womanist Criticism/Theology
Alice Walker, seeking an alternative word for organizing people’s
thinking about black women’s “self-definitions, relationships, activi -

the term “womanist” in 1982. In her book Sisters in the Wilderness,
Delores William defines womanist theology in the following way:
Womanist theology is a prophetic voice concerned about the well-being
of the entire African American community, male and female, adults and

children. Womanist theology attempts to help black women see, affirm,
and have confidence in the importance of their experience and faith for

church and society . (4) It is committed to reason and black women’s ex-

Methods Involving More than One World
Appendix
IV
METHODS INVOLVING MORE THAN
ONE WORLD
M
eaning results from an active conversation between the world of
the text and the world of the reader, a conversation informed by
the world of the author. The locus of meaning is not to be found exclu-
sively in either world or in a marriage of any two of the worlds, but
in the interplay between all three worlds.
Ideological Criticism
Ideological criticism attempts to uncover the ideology of a text and
the ideological influences during the history of its interpretation. Ide-
ology refers to the “social structures marked by patterns of dominance
and subordination, i.e., race, ethnicity, gender, religion, etc.”
1 The cen-
tral focus of ideological criticism is to offer an approach to examining
texts that asks readers to be aware of and account for not only the ideo-
logical frameworks of the author and the text, but their own ideological
frameworks. Consequently, there are at least three areas to which ideo-
logical critics may direct their attention: (1) the ideological context in
which an author produces a text, (2) the ideology reproduced within
the text itself, and (3) the ideology of a text’s readers.
An author’s ideology, consisting of beliefs and assumptions on such
issues as social class, economics, gender, and race, affect the way in
which he or she writes, not to mention the content of the writing. Thus
the reader can better understand the text by attempting to grasp the
content of an author’s ideology. Additionally, many hold to the notion
that the text itself implies a certain ideology. Again, the astute reader
will look for such things as the oppression of groups or the patriarchal
1 Soulen and Soulen, Handbook of Biblical Criticism , 84.

--- Page 347 ---
dominance of women. Finally, readers must be aware of their own
ideological biases that they bring to the reading of a text.
Although ideological criticism is varied in expression, the com -
mon thread running through all its expressions is the assumption that
texts are not value-neutral but, rather, reflect to different degrees the
relations and structures of race, gender, and class, which empower
some persons and disenfranchise othe rs. Ideological criticism, there -
fore, demands that interpreters of texts not only read from a posture of
suspicion, but also that they critically examine their own interpretive
traditions. In other words, in ideological criticism, readers cannot di -
vorce themselves from ethical issues that become inherent in the act
of interpretation.

Aichele, George et al. The Postmodern Bible. New Haven: Yale Univer-

Intertextual Criticism
Intertextual criticism is based upon Julia Kristeva’s term “inter -
textuality,” which she introduced in 1969 in a critique of the modern
assumptions that texts contain meaning and that critics can objectively
excavate that meaning. Kristeva also used the term “text” to include
anything that might be read, such as a text, person, ritual, or anything
in culture that must be interpreted. According to Kristeva, a text is a
centerless convergence of various signifying systems, an intersection of
other texts. Every text is a conjunction of other texts of which it is a re-
326 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS

--- Page 348 ---
reading.2 So a text is a kind of patchwork quilt constructed from pieces
of other texts, which are themselves constructed through the inter -
weaving of other texts and discourses.
It becomes a truism, then, that interpretations can never exhaust
what may be said about texts. A single interpretation can never be the
last word, but simply one possible way among an infinite number of
ways to configure the indeterminacy of the text’s intertextuality. This
means that any interpretation (or method of interpretation, for that
matter) exists only at the expense of excluding other possibilities. To
use the terminology of Umberto Eco, any interpretation results from a
process of “blowing up” parts of the text while “narcoticizing” other
parts.
3 In other words, interpretation is a matter of tracing intertextual
relations guided by ideological and methodological agenda, personal
interests, and affiliatory expectations (academic, theological, philo -
sophical, etc.). The object of any textual analysis, then, is not necessar-
ily meaning in terms of the relationship between the language of the
text and an extratextual referent but signification in the sense of unlim-
ited semiosis, that is, the relationship of signs and texts to other signs
and texts as indeterminate objects.
Some critics, however, have extended the definition of intertextual
criticism to mean something much broader. For example, Donald Kee-
sey
4 sees intertextual criticism as having a twofold focus. First, he in-
cludes what other critics call genre criticism under the umbrella of
intertextual criticism. Accordingly, literary texts do not imitate life but
other texts of the same genre. Authors do not transform reality but
transform the conventions of the genre within which they are working.
For example, in writing theAeneid, Virgil expected his readers to recog-
nize that he was writing an epic that would look a lot like Homer’sOd-
yssey. While his epic is structurally very similar to Homer’s, it is an
interpretive appropriation of the Odyssey. If Virgil’s readers did not rec-
ognize this appropriation, they would be unable to grasp his purpose.
While any single work may not conform exactly to its genre, it would be
unintelligible if it were absolutely unique. So, when a reader analyzes a
Methods Involving More than One World 327
2 Julia Kristeva, “The Bounded Text,” in Desire in Language: A Semiotic
Approach to Literature and Art ( t r a n s .T h o m a sG o r ae ta l . ;N e wY o r k :C o l u m-
bia University Press, 1980), 36.
3 Umberto Eco, The Role of the Reader: Explorations in the Semiotics of Texts
(Bloomington: Indiana University Press, 1979), 228.
4 See Donald Keesey, “Intertextual Criticism: Literature as Context,” in
Contexts for Criticism(4th ed.; ed. Donald Keesey; Boston: McGraw Hill, 2003),
265–77.

--- Page 349 ---
text by relating it to other texts of the same genre, the reader is practic-
ing a type of intertextual criticism. A parable, for example, is best read
in light of all other parables ever written. Similarly, the best reading of
the wisdom literature in the Hebrew Bible occurs in the context of all
other wisdom literature.
The last observation points to the second focus of intertextual
criticism—the reciprocal nature of intertextuality. First, since texts
are constructed from prior texts, the most plausible reading of any text
is the one which recognizes an author’s intertexts and the manner in
which the author appropriates these texts. For example, a reading
that takes into account Mark’s appropriation of texts from the He -
brew Bible is an intertextual reading. Since Mark recontextualizes
these texts, a reading that does not or cannot recognize such appro -
priation is impoverished. This idea of one author appropriating or
alluding to other texts has become an important part of biblical inter -
textual criticism. The interest in the idea of intertexts is reflected in
recent studies that claim that the authors of the Synoptic Gospels
used Greco-Roman epics such as Homer’s Iliad and Odyssey and Vir-
gil’s Aeneid (see McDonald; Bonz) as intertexts. If the appropriated
texts are themselves networks of appropriated texts, then the act of
interpretation potentially extends indefinitely. There simply is no back-
ward boundary for the interpretive process. Interpretation may end,
b u ti ti sn e v e rf i n i s h e d .
Intertextual reciprocity also extends into the indefinite future. Not
only do former texts inform the reading of later texts; the reversal is
true as well. That is, reading later texts informs the reading of former
texts. For example, one’s reading of Alexander Pope’sRape of the Lock
and John Milton’s Paradise Lost makes for a more plausible reading of
Virgil’sAeneid, which in turn informs one’s reading of Homer’sOdyssey.
If some modern critics are correct in their claim that the author of Luke-
Acts constructed his work in conversation with Greco-Roman epic (es-
pecially Virgil’s Aeneid ), then one’s reading of Luke-Acts would be
greatly enhanced by reading epics such asThe Rape of the Lock and Par-
adise Lost, which were written centuries after Luke-Acts. Consequently,
when a text becomes an intertext for other texts, its literary significa -
tion extends beyond itself. The intertextual critic, therefore, is also in -
terested in examining how other texts have appropriated a given text
and then using this information in a reexamination of the appropriated
text. Clearly then, a text is really never completed but is always in the
process of being written; it is always in an intertextual process of pro -
duction, which includes both past and future texts.
328 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS

--- Page 350 ---
So intertextual criticism may focus on the issue of genre and lin-
guistic and literary conventions, but it will always be concerned with
the reciprocity of intertextuality and indeterminacy , which it naturally
inscribes. Michael Riffaterre sums up intertextuality by defining it as
the relationship between readers and their recognition of a text’s rela-
tionship to texts that precede or follow it.

Marxist Criticism
The Marxist critic holds that art serves as an ideological mecha -
nism, legitimating and perpetuating the system of social values, beliefs,
and relationships by which one class dominates the others. Central to
Marxism is the assumption that individual human consciousness, cul-
tural experience, and even society itself are products of discrete, ma -
terial processes and can be understood in scientific terms. For Marx
and Engels, the founding fathers of Marxism, the underlying dynamic
of social change was the struggle for dominance between economic
classes. Every change in the economic structure of a society was invari-
ably accompanied by a corresponding change to its social order. This
relationship led them to believe that the economic form in which one
class has power over the others determines the ways in which the social
hierarchy develops.
In recent Western history, for example, this class struggle has taken
the form of capitalism. In a capitalistic economy, the modes of produc-
tion are controlled by a ruling class but operated by a working class.
Because the modes of production—the way in which production is car-
ried out and the structures that control it—are owned by the ruling class,
workers are dependent upon them for survival. In other words, self-
sufficiency is not so much a result of an individual’s ability to procure
material necessities as it is an individual’s ability to enter into a relation-
ship with those who control the distribution of material necessities.
However, in order for this system to work, the number of workers
must exceed the number of jobs. There must be a measure of un -
employment. Because there are more workers than jobs, laborers are
forced to compete with each other for employment. Competition for
jobs, in turn, drives down job wages because those who have no work
become willing to work for less in order to obtain employment. In this
way, workers are not paid for the full value of their labor. In some of
the less regulated forms of capitalism, this wage atrophy advances to
the point where a harmony is struck between the minimum cost of sur-
vival and a worker’s income, reducing the working force into what has
been called “wage slaves.” In the end, an artificial disparity is created
between the market value of a product and the actual cost of produc -
tion. This artificial disparity is called the surplus value of labor and is
absorbed by the bourgeoisie as profit by mere virtue of the fact that they
own the means of production and are able to leverage this economic
ordering to their benefit. The individual worker, in order to survive,
330 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS

--- Page 352 ---
has no choice but to participate in this exploitive social structuring and
is essentially powerless to remedy his or her situation.
Marx and Engels also asserted that the social, cultural, and physi -
cal environment one is born into determines, to a large degree, the de-
velopment of the individual consciousness. In other words, society
creates the individual rather than the other way around.
If the social structure of a society is in some way determined by
its economic structure, then consciousness derives ultimately from that
economic structure as well. This synthesis between the views of the eco-
nomic basis of social structure and the development of consciousness
results in a bipartite formulation of society according to which society
is composed of two divisions: the base, which is the economic struc -
ture, and the superstructure, the collective social consciousness that de-
velops in the context of the economic base. It is much like an all-
inclusive ideology or worldview and includes such things as religion,
morality, aesthetics, philosophy, politics, and law .
However, because the superstructure derives from an exploitive eco-
nomic base, it creates a false consciousness that functions to legitimate
the existing social structures. In other words, it is “the complex structure
of social perception which ensures that the situation in which one class
has power over the others is seen as ‘natural’ or not seen at all.”
6
The base and superstructure are so interrelated that changes within
one will have recursive effects upon both itself and in the other. Con-
sciousness is not only an object within a particular environment; it is
also an integral element in the total composition of that environment.
However inextricably bound the two components are, it is ultimately
the base that determines the nature of the superstructure, since it is the
base that grants one class power over the other classes. The superstruc-
ture merely exists to obscure the fact that the exploited classes are the
victims of exploitation.
Marxist criticism is an application of the theory of superstructure
to art, in which art itself exhibits in its very composition the ideology
of the dominant class. According to Terry Eagleton, a leading if not
the foremost neo-Marxist critic, ideology, as it is used here,
is not in the first place a set of doctrines; it signifies the way men live out
their roles in class-society, the values, ideas and images which tie them
to their social functions and so prevent them from a true knowledge of
s o c i e t ya saw h o l e .I nt h i ss e n s e...i ts h o w sam a nm a k i n gs e n s eo fh i s
Methods Involving More than One World 331
6 Terry Eagleton, Marxism and Literary Criticism (Berkeley: University of
California Press, 1976), 5.

--- Page 353 ---
experience in ways that prohibit a true understanding of his society, ways
that are consequently false.7
Marxist criticism is concerned with the complex relations uniting
a work of literature to the ideology in which it originated. A non-
simplistic understanding of a work must not overlook the several ele -
ments of the base-and-superstructure model—an author’s class position,
ideological forms and their relation to literary forms, spirituality and
philosophy, techniques of literary production, aesthetic theory, and the
economic base—which typify a period and how these elements con -
verge to produce a literary specimen. A literary work, after all, is an
elaborate concoction of values, images, and ideas specific to a time.
The Marxist wishes to expose the falsities and unnatural arrange -
ments that endorse one class’ power over the others. For the Marxist,
all literature can be scrutinized for ideological ingredients, but good
literature will typify the ideology of its period in such a way that ideol-
ogy becomes consciously visible rather than just unconsciously present.
Good literature will communicate a sense of realism and faithfulness
to the social experience while exposing and implicitly challenging the
limitedness, inadequacy, and artificiality of the very real structures
that have been imposed upon it. In this way, it is able to generate a uni-
versal appeal and relevancy to all readers across time while being firmly
rooted in its own context. In the end, a Marxist critique examines
all aspects of a piece of literature—from form and style to characteri-
zation and plot, and even to the mode of production in which it was
published—for social authenticity in order to expose the falseness of
prevailing ideologies.
Approaching it from a non-theistic point of view , the Marxist sees
the Bible as any other text subject to the same criticisms as all other
texts. From a Marxist point of view , it would be difficult to construe
the traditional ideas of inerrancy and infallibility as anything but pil -
lars in the ideological mechanism sanctioning the structures that em -
power sacred authority. More devastating to an individual’s hope of
eschewing these social structures is the concept of original sin, whereby
the lot of humanity is consigned to eternal punishment. The only es -
cape, of course, is obsequious submission to the Almighty’s authority,
which he has, in his ever merciful wisdom, seen fit to vest in the institu-
tion of the church.
Eagleton has applied these techniques to an interpretation of the
biblical book of Jonah in his essay “J. L. Austin and the Book of
332 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS
7 Ibid., 17.

--- Page 354 ---
Jonah.”8 According to Eagleton’s essay, God sends Jonah to prophesy
to Nineveh because God is really too maudlin to destroy the city him-
self and needs an excuse to spare the city in order to save face. Jonah
sees right through God’s self-deception and realizes that his own part in
the narrative is only superficial. Consequently, Jonah is disturbed by what
he judges to be God’s manipulative motives, especially when Nineveh
miraculously repents. Eagleton further concludes that the source of
Jonah’s despair is his recognition that as long as God is providentially
guiding and directing everything, individual human action is meaning-
less. But then Jonah thinks that God may have put him up to this
task just to make him question the meaningfulness of action. He real -
izes that he cannot even know if he knows whether action is meaning-
less or not. This realization plunges Jonah from existential despair into
aimless subjectivism. The story ends with God reproaching Jonah’s
despair suggesting, according to Eagleton, that despite the prophet’s
earlier assessment, the divine propensity to mercy is, in fact, fickle and
absurd.
Eagleton is painting Jonah’s plight against the ideological back-
drop of history. Jonah’s relation to God, in this interpretation, is an al-
legory of the individual’s relation to the Marxist conception of history.
To what degree does human action matter if action is inseparable from
history and that which is acted upon? And to what degree can we even
know if human action matters? By raising these questions, Eagleton
entices the reader to glimpse the individual’s inescapable connection to
history and the subjectivity that results.
Such as it is, this interpretation is not a traditional Marxist reading
of the book of Jonah, but then again Eagleton is not a traditional
Marxist. What is important is the insight provided by the illumination
of ideological elements—the conjunction of Jonah’s well-founded re -
luctance, the meaninglessness of his action, his subsequent despair,
and God’s ever present and unpredictable “otherness” present a realis-
tic image of social experience characteristic of false consciousness.
Eagleton summarizes this dilemma: “The Book of Jonah leaves its
protagonist caught in a transitional stage between false consciousness
and some new , currently unnamable style of identity; and to this extent
we have made little advance upon it.”

Mimetic Criticism
Mimetic criticism finds the meaning of a text in the relationship
between the text and reality. Almost any verbal representation seems
to force some type of comparison with our experience. The mimetic
critic, then, judges the worth of a literary text by its ability to represent
or reflect some aspect of the world outside the text. Good literature,
claims the mimetic critic, reveals some truth about the way humans ex-
perience nature. In one way or another, all mimetic critics appeal to a
shared human common sense or experience of the world and the text’s
imitation of that common sense or experience as the locus of meaning.
From the time of the ancient Greeks to the eighteenth century, the
idea that literature was essentially mimetic was an unchallenged prem-
ise. Literature revealed truth about life.
10 In his Poetics, Aristotle argued
not only that the heart of tragedy was its catharsis of pity and fear but
that tragedy produced in the viewer or reader an insight experience.
Such an experience was what the reader or viewer of the tragedy rec -
ognized as the truth that the text revealed about some aspect of the
human condition, i.e., an insight into some universal aspect of a reality
external to the text itself. This is the reason that Aristotle can claim that
art is more closely allied with philosophy than with history—because
like philosophy, art seeks the universal rather than the particular.
334 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS
10 Donald Keesey, “Mimetic Criticism: Reality as Context,” in Contexts
for Criticism (4th ed.; ed. Donald Keesey; Boston: McGraw Hill, 2003), 205.

--- Page 356 ---
Mimetic critics claim that the mimetic relationship between reality,
defined as the infinite flux of changing experience, and a text is the
complexity of both. While the text may be formal in design and does
not replicate reality, it does mirror it in the complexity of the design. The
experience of reading a complex text is similar to our experience of life.
We attempt to make sense of both by imposing an interpretive grid, in
terms of a system of thought, upon them.
Mimetic critics also claim that characterization, setting, plot, and
action in literature invite a comparison with life as we experience it
outside the text. So, says the mimetic critic, if artistic representations
invite comparison with non-artistic experience, literature forces us to
ask important questions about life.
Mimetic criticism seems to correspond well to the way that many
readers and critics approach the biblical texts and to the way that the
Bible as literature courses in higher education approach the texts. When
readers of the book of Job ask questions about the relationship be-
tween piety and divine blessing, they are assuming that Job suggests
something theologically significant. When a biblical scholar suggests
that the Gospel of Mark is redefining the concept of messiahship, he
or she is assuming that the gospel employs literary strategies, form, and
content to suggest something about the way we should view an aspect
of life, or when a reader seeks to find guidance in the Psalms or Prov-
erbs, he or she is reading mimetically.

Keesey, Donald, ed. Contexts for Criticism. 4th ed.; Boston: McGraw-
Hill, 2003.
Tate, W . Randolph.Interpreting the Bible: A Handbook of Terms and Meth-
ods. Peabody, Mass.: Hendrickson, 2006.
Narrative Criticism
Narrative criticism is the study of narratives, focusing not only on
traditional narrative elements such as plot, setting, and characteriza -
tion, but also the role of the reader. Like reader-response criticism, nar-
rative criticism assumes that the story does not exist autonomously
within the text, but comes into being through the interaction between
the text and the reader. Narrative critics treat the narrative itself as the
signifier and the story (produced through the interaction between the
Methods Involving More than One World 335

--- Page 357 ---
narrative and reader) as the signified. Narrative criticism is the analysis
of the narrative content within the context of relationships between au-
thors, texts, and readers (see diagram on p. 96).
Narrative critics identify a literary entity who is found only within
the text. This entity is not the real author, but only an incomplete re -
flection of the real author. For example, when I read the Gospel of
Mark, the only author I encounter is that one which I may be able to
construct from the text alone. Even more revealing is the reading of sev-
eral Pauline epistles. When I read 1 Corinthians, I encounter one entity;
when I read Galatians, I encounter another. These two implied authors
are not the same because each text yields different information. Conse-
quently, it is possible for a real-world person to author several texts,
each one having a different implied author. Another way to put it is that
the implied author is a composite of discernible ideologies underlying
a particular text.
The implied reader, like the implied author, is defined by the text.
The implied author may consciously or unconsciously construct the
text in such a manner as to provide guidance to a reader. This means
that a text contains unspoken assumptions about the reader. It is obvi-
ous that the implied author (the real author for that matter) of Mark’s
Gospel did not have a twentieth-century reader in mind when he or she
wrote. Even though the text provides direction for the reader by giving
definition to the implied reader, the implied reader is not entirely de-
fined by the text. The implied author may assume on the part of the
implied reader a set of values, literary competence, and background.
But there is simply no way to assure that every reader will satisfy this
role. On the one hand, even if we assume that the role of the implied
reader may be completely defined, no single reader can perfectly and
completely conform to the role the text provides for the implied reader.
On the other hand, to the degree that the reader fulfills the role of the
implied reader provided for by the text, the reader becomes captive to
the text and its ideology. But the opposite may also occur, i.e., a reader
may discover that the role demanded of the implied reader is so foreign
or ideologically distasteful that the reader simply refuses to read, thus
rejecting the role of the implied reader.
Since all narratives are told through the voice of a narrator, the per-
son and activity of the narrator occupy a place of special importance
in the determination of meaning. A narrative may be told in the first or
third person, and the narrator may be outside the action or a character
within the story. Further, a narrator may vary in degree of knowledge,
from very limited knowledge to virtual omniscience.
336 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS

--- Page 358 ---
Furthermore, the narrator is not identical to the implied author. The
implied author creates the fictive narrator who is dependent upon the
implied author for any characteristics and abilities which may be dis -
cernible from the text. The view of the narrator may or may not reflect
the ideology of the implied author. This is true of the third person omni-
scient narrator or of the narrator who is also a character within the story.
The counterpart of the narrator is the narratee, the entity to whom
the narrator tells the story. While the narratee receives the story as it un-
folds moment by moment, the implied reader reads what the narrator
tells the narratee. The implied reader is an entity with a set of values
that enables him or her to complete the work. The narratee, however,
has total definition only by the work. Like the narrator, the narratee may
be a character within the story, but is usually a person who has no place
within the story. The identity of the narratee will determine to a large
degree the style and complexity of the narrator’s language. If the nar -
ratee is uninformed about the subject matter, the narrator may become
very detailed, offering extensive explanation.
In narrative criticism the narrative world is not identical to the real
world. The real world of both author and reader alike is infinite, but ob-
s e r v a b l ea n dp r e s e n t ,w i t ha l le v e n t sw o r k i n gt o g e t h e ri na ni n t e r c o n -
nected system. The narrative world is both limited and bounded, the
only access to which is the secondary medium of the text. The objects,
persons, and events in the narrative may not correspond to anything in
the real world. In order for the implied reader to reconstruct the narra-
tive world, determinations as to relative importance of events must be
made. The implied author highlights some events, subordinates others,
and leaves gaps while moving from one narrative segment to another.
The responsibility of bridging these gaps belongs to the implied reader.
According to the narrative critic, meaning begins with the narra -
tive world, not the real world. The meaning of the Gospel of Mark is
not to be found in considering Mark an objective, historically accurate
portrayal of the life and times of Jesus. What Jesus (or any character
for that matter) says or does in the narrative must find its significance
within the world of the narrative alone. The implied author shapes the
story world by selecting some events and excluding others. This pro -
cess of selectivity immediately excludes the possibility of the narrative
world being a complete replica of the real one. All meaning, therefore,
must be constructed in conversation with the narrative world, which is
a construct of the text. Meaning must be a function of the relation -
ships, experiences, and connections which the implied reader is called
upon to create.
Methods Involving More than One World 337

--- Page 359 ---

Powell, Mark Allen. What is Narrative Criticism? Minneapolis: Fortress,

Tate, W . Randolph. Interpreting the Bible: A Handbook of Terms and
Methods. Peabody, Mass.: Hendrickson, 2006.
Socio-Rhetorical Criticism
Socio-rhetorical criticism is an interpretive method defined by
Vernon Robbins as “a textually-based method that uses programmatic
strategies to invite social, cultural, historical, psychological, aesthetic,
ideological and theological information into a context of minute exe -
getical activity.”
11 For the socio-rhetorical critic, the focus of inter -
pretation is the text as a cultural, social, ideological, historical, and
theological discourse, and the data from these various areas have an
intertextual relation with respect to the textual signs.
12 The socio-
rhetorical critic, then, understands the term “rhetorical” to refer to the
text as a literary object, i.e., a story that must be read, and the term
“socio” as referring to the text as a cultural artifact that must be opened
“to the past, present, and future.”
13 From this posture, Robbins has ar-
ticulated a fourfold framework within which socio-rhetorical criticism
works: (1) rhetorical-literary aspects of the text; (2) the intertextuality of
the text; (3) the social and cultural dynamics operating in the text; and
(4) ideology. Later, however, Robbins restates the fourfold framework
of the criticism based upon the various textures of the text and claims
that readers of texts will focus on one texture at the expense of the oth-
ers. The four textures are (1) inner texture, (2) intertexture, (3) social and
cultural texture, and (4) ideological texture. In a later publication, Ex-
ploring the Texture of Texts, Robbins adds a fifth texture—sacred texture.
Inner texture is the entry level of analysis and focuses on the fea -
tures of the text’s language, including such things as word patterns (e.g.,
repetition of words and the sequencing of terms); textual units; form
of argument; voices; literary and rhetorical devices; and the mixing of
338 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS
11 Vernon K. Robbins “Socio-Rhetorical Criticism: Mary, Elizabeth, and
the Magnificat as a Test Case,” inThe New Literary Criticism and the New Testa-
ment (ed. Edgar V . McKnight and Elizabeth Struthers Malbon; Valley Forge:
Trinity Press International, 1994), 165.
12 Ibid., 166.
13 Robbins, Jesus the Teacher, xxiii.

--- Page 360 ---
speech and narrative.14 Robbins identifies six types of inner texture: (1)
repetitive, in which a word or phrase occurs multiple times in a single
unit; (2) progressive, in terms of the sequencing of words or phrases
in a unit, such as the alternation of words (e.g., the language of king -
ship in Mark 15:1–16:8) and word chains; (3) narrational, which en -
compasses the various voices in the text (narratorial voice, reported
speech, written texts, characters’ voices); (4) opening, middle and clos-
ing of the unit; (5) argumentative, the argument of the text in terms of
both modern and ancient rhetorical theory; and (6) sensory-aesthetic,
which is concerned with the ways in which the language of the text
evokes the senses, emotion, and thought patterns of readers (issues
of genre on the level of text and subtexts become important in this
texture).
15
Intertexture is concerned with the manner in which the author in -
terprets and presents material outside the text within the text itself.
Two important questions guide this type of analysis: “From where has
this passage adopted its language? With what texts does this text stand
in dialogue?”
16 Robbins describes five dimensions of intertexture anal-
ysis: analysis of reference, recitation, recontextualization, reconfigura-
tion, and echo.
17
The first dimension of intertexture is reference to persons, objects,
places, traditions, and texts. In other words, analysis of reference is con-
cerned with the things with which the text is in dialogue and with the
ways that interpreters decide what traditions and texts are referenced
and the significance that those determinations have.
The second dimension is recitation, for instance, the manner in which
a speech is reported—exact language, modified language, or different lan-
guage altogether from the original. Recitation also deals with whether
an account is summarized, expanded, or just what episode or event is
referenced. Robbins argues that the uncertainty of references leaves
the interpreter a certain amount of freedom to determine the reference,
a determination that may be ideologically motivated. Consequently,
the interpreter may foreground one possibility while negating others.
Recontextualization refers to the practice of the placement of nar -
ratives, speeches, etc., in new contexts without announcing the fact.
Methods Involving More than One World 339
14 Vernon Robbins, Exploring the Texture of Texts: A Guide to Socio-
rhetorical Interpretation (Harrisburg: Trinity Press International, 1996), 3–7.
15 Ibid., 8–30.
16 Ibid., 179.
17 Ibid., 179–85; see also 40–50.

--- Page 361 ---
For example, Robbins refers to Raymond E. Brown’sBirth of the Messiah,
in which he points out a significant number of recontextualized speeches
from the LXX in Luke 1:26–56.
With respect to reconfiguration, Robbins argues that authors recon-
figure both texts and well-known traditions. For example, in the Magni-
ficat, Luke may have reconfigured the Israelite tradition of the barren
woman who conceives a child at an advanced age. However, given both
the Judaic and Hellenistic influence on Luke’s gospel, the reconfigura-
tions may have multiple souces, e.g., the stories of virgins in the
LXX and
the Greco-Roman stories of women imprenated by gods.18
The final dimension is intertextual echo, which goes beyond recon-
textualization and reconfiguration to include soundings from tradition
and culture. Given that the New Testament texts were written within a
Mediterranean culture that was multifaceted, socio-rhetorical critics de-
mand that all cultural traditions be brought to bear on the analysis of
texts. For example, on the one hand, scholars have compared the Mag-
nificat with the praise hymns in the Psalms. On the other hand, however,
Robbins asks about the frequency with which scholars are willing to
compare the Magnificat with hymns of praise in Roman-Hellenistic lit-
erature as well as other Mediterranean literature.
Social and cultural texture involves theories of both anthropology
and sociology. The primary focus here is upon “the social and culture
nature of the text.”
19 While the text is an artifact of a particular culture
and social location, it is also a response to that culture and social loca-
tion. Robbins delineates the parameters of this texture in terms of
“specific social topics, common social and cultural topics, and final
cultural categories.”
20
Common social and cultural topics are those that persons in a par-
ticular culture are familiar with instinctively. When an interpreter can
access these topics, the extent of interpretive anachronism is dimin -
ished. In first-century Mediterranean texts, the interpreter will be faced
with social structures of honor/shame, clean/unclean, purity/impurity,
codes of argumentation, systems of legality, economic exchange sys -
tems based upon a variety of subsystems (e.g., agriculture, business),
and hierarchical social structures.
The ideological texture is based upon the assumption that every
text inscribes an ideology, a body of political, economic, religious, or
340 SYNOPSES OF ADDITIONAL INTERPRETIVE METHODS
18 Vernon Robbins, “Socio-Rhetorical Criticism,” 180.
19 Robbins, Exploring the Texture of Texts,71.
20 Ibid.

--- Page 362 ---
social ideas upon which a culture’s values, thoughts, judgments, etc.,
are based. Within ideological criticism, ideology may refer to three
contexts: (1) the ideological context in which an author produces a text,
(2) the ideology reproduced within the text itself, and (3) the ideology
of a text’s readers or “customers.” Socio-rhetorical criticism examines
all three ideological contexts but tends to focus on the first two. By ex-
amining the artifacts (including textual ones), scholars can partially re-
construct the ideology of a culture.
Much of the work of anthropologists and sociologists focuses on
this reconstruction. To reconstruct the ideology represented by the text
or, perhaps better, to reconstruct the ideology underlying the text, socio-
rhetorical critics must analyze the rhetorical structures of language.
Robbins suggests that certain questions should be asked: “What and
whose self-interests are being negotiated in this text? If the dominant
voices in the text persuade people to act according to their premises,
who will gain and who will lose? What will be gained and what will be
lost? . . . Whose ideology is being advanced, for whose benefit?”
21
From the perspective of the socio-rhetorical critic, then, every text is
ideological and to at least some degree propagandistic. If we assume,
therefore, that early Christianity had its politics or ideological posi-
tions, then we may also assume that its texts are ideological.
By the “sacred texture” of texts Robbins means not only what the
text says about the divine but also the ways that readers explore the sa-
cred nature of texts. He recommends that since the sacred texture is
embedded within the other four, a more plausible understanding of the
text develops as the interpreter allows the sacred nature to emerge from
a detailed examination of the first four textures. This idea of the sacred
is located in those references in the text to deity , holy persons, spirit be-
ings, divine history , human redemption, human commitment, religious
community , and ethics.
22 Again these elements emerge from a multi-
textured analysis of the text.
Socio-rhetorical critics recognize that written and oral communi-
cation and their interpretations are social acts in which authors, speak-
ers, and readers all stand squarely within meaning-making ideologies
and communities. Consequently , texts and their interpreters comprise
a multi-contextual location with multiple boundaries. Socio-rhetorical
criticism responds to this complex labyrinth by drawing from a variety
of interpretive approaches in order to configure readings that account
